## 2. §3 in my own words

**Intent.** Today a dispatch's shell is held for the scan after its worker starts — the
post-launch repository tree snapshot, one `git status` per registered worktree, 244 × 3.3 s on
reckon — so each dispatch leaves a stray monitor beside the follower. §3 removes the hold by
moving the work the dispatch does around the launch: a dispatch prints its JSON result and
exits once its worker runs and the live pointer is written; a small detached per-run supervisor
takes over the boundary baseline, the spawn, the wait and the exit record. The worker must
survive the dispatch process ending by SIGTERM, SIGKILL or SIGHUP — pinned as signals to the
dispatch's pid alone, never its group, which is how a host expiring a background shell ends it.
The snapshot itself is a boundary baseline: it must follow dispatch's own writes and predate
the worker's first write in any other tree, and the revision settles that the supervisor takes
the snapshot after dispatch's writes and before it spawns the worker. A snapshot that raises is
recorded at `snapshot-error.txt` in the run directory, the worker still spawns, the pointer
carries `{"available": false, "detail": ...}`, and a snapshot failure never kills a worker and
never turns a launch into a refusal. The exit collection moves to the supervisor rather than to
init, which is why a worker reparented to init loses its exit today (0 exit records across
every live pointer after a night of at least eight deaths). One record serves a death and a
launch failure: `exit.json` in the run directory, copied under the pointer's `exit` key while
the pointer exists, with the nine fields of the table plus `ended_during`; `ended_during` reads
`launch` exactly when `stream_records_seen` is 0 and is stated because a worker that never
started and one that died while working need different recovery. The supervisor updates the
pointer only over an existing file and never creates one, so a discarded pointer stays
discarded, and liveness stays read from the pointer's recorded worker pid by
`record_process_alive`. The record's name outside the run directory is `worker_exit`, because
`exit_status` already holds the gate command's status in every ledger row that carries it, and
a record kept only on the pointer is destroyed by promotion. Carrying it into the ledger row
is §7.

**Done-when, in my own words.** `tests/test_dispatch_returns_without_worker.py`, four cases,
stub backend, temporary repository and temporary `RECKON_HOME`. (1) The dispatch exits within
five seconds carrying the run id in its JSON, nothing is killed, and the stub's marker file
appears; this case's repository holds at least 30 registered worktrees so a worktree-count scan
is visible in the time. (2) SIGKILL to the dispatch process before it returns still leaves the
marker file appearing. (3) The worker-death-is-recorded §5 measure, verbatim: a real dispatch,
the dispatching process gone, SIGKILL, the exit status still recorded — realised here as
dispatch returned, the worker SIGKILLed, and within ten seconds both the run directory's exit
record and the pointer carrying the exit status with signal name `SIGKILL`. (4) The pointer is
discarded before the worker exits; after the exit no pointer exists while the run directory's
exit record does. Two negative controls are executed and logged: putting the tree snapshot back
between the launch and dispatch's return makes case 1 fail on the five-second bound, and
removing the supervisor's wait makes case 3 fail; both logs are kept with the manifest. The
real pointer directory under the user's config home is asserted untouched. Write paths:
`reckon/crew/dispatch.py` and the test.

## 3. §4 in my own words

**Intent.** Once §3 lands a dispatch is an ordinary short command, so no instruction may tell a
coordinator to background a CLI dispatch or to yield after it; a coordinator runs it in the
foreground and reads its JSON. The follower remains the only long-lived process a session
watches — §3's per-run supervisors are detached and belong to their runs, so a session neither
watches nor waits on them. Every instruction that says to background a CLI dispatch or to
yield after it is changed, across `skills/reckon-ship/SKILL.md`,
`skills/reckon-ship/references/orchestrator-harness/claude-code.md` and
`skills/reckon-ship/references/sprint-orchestration.md` (the third file is the revision's
addition, paging the scout note at line 684 back into scope). The section runs after §3 merges.

**Done-when, in my own words.** The launch-kind table in `skills/reckon-ship/SKILL.md` says a
CLI dispatch returns once its worker runs and is run in the foreground. The harness reference
names the follower as the session's only monitor. No sentence in `skills/` instructs
backgrounding a `reckon crew dispatch`, checked by reading `grep -rn "dispatch" skills/` line
by line, with the lines that mention backgrounding listed in the manifest — a literal grep for
"background" is explicitly not the measure. Write paths: the two named files and
`sprint-orchestration.md`.

## 4. §5 in my own words

**Intent.** imas-ambix-b6's coordinator read a refusal's live pointer as a death and removed a
worktree on the strength of it — a refusal had been returned after a live pointer had been
written; the pointer read `abandoned` / "the process is gone without a complete manifest" four
seconds after the dispatch. §5's rule: find where a refusal can occur after the pointer is
written, and either remove the pointer on refusal or order the refusal list so every refusal
precedes the write. The revision adds the dispatch contract's reproduce-first shape: at the
current main every refusal path in `dispatch` may already precede the pointer write, so the
node establishes with a test whether *any* refusal still leaves a pointer or a worktree, and
if none does, the section closes with the test added and no source change — the negative
control is logged only against a refusal path that does leave a trace at the base revision. A
related observed case is the discard that left a launch-failed review's worktree on disk, so
a re-dispatch of the same node id was refused `worktree path already exists`: the refusal is
correct, but the leftover worktree is what a discard should not leave.

**Done-when, in my own words.** `tests/test_refused_dispatch_leaves_no_pointer.py` forces
**each** refusal `dispatch` can return after worktree creation starts, in a temporary
`RECKON_HOME` and repository. For each it asserts no live pointer and no worktree remain for
that node, and that the watcher's transition stream for that home carries no row naming it. The
manifest lists every refusal path exercised; if none leaves a trace at base the manifest says
so and no source change is owed; if one does, the negative control is executed and logged
against that path at the base revision. Write paths: `reckon/crew/dispatch.py` and that test.

## 5. §6 in my own words

**Intent.** imas-efit-e3 measured a refusal returned for a dispatch that had launched: two
dispatches of one node were refused (`not-dispatchable`, then `dispatch-refused` for the
existing worktree path) while the run's pointer was live and its stream was eleven seconds old
— so a worker was running. Such a refusal invites a duplicate dispatch of a working node and
the removal of a live process's worktree. §6's rule: find the path where a refusal is returned
after the pointer is written or the worker is spawned, and make the result agree with what
happened — either success naming the launched run, or nothing launched at all. Separately,
establish why an explicit `--member` was replaced by an auto-derived member, and refuse rather
than substitute when the named member cannot be bound. The exit status is not the defect here:
a `dispatch-refused` exits 1, a contract-validation refusal exits 2, and the zeros that were
reported came from pipes. One part of the correction is a defect — the contract-validation
refusal returns `ok: false` with `error` absent/null, carrying its reason only under
`validation.findings`, while the skill documents that every refusal carries `error` and
`detail`. The candidate cause is the exception handler around the post-launch tree snapshot,
which signals the launched worker's process group and returns a refusal; §3 moves that snapshot
into the supervisor, so the node reproduces at the base revision and states whether §3's change
already removes the path. The read-back found a named `--member` has been refused rather than
substituted since 2026-08-24, so the member test verifies existing behaviour and changes no
source.

**Done-when, in my own words.** `tests/test_dispatch_result_matches_launch.py` does three
things. (1) It drives a dispatch through the failing path with a stub backend in a temporary
crew config home and asserts that the result either names the launched run with `ok: true` or
leaves no pointer and no process. (2) A second test passes an unresolvable `--member` and
asserts a refusal with no auto-derived member bound — verifying behaviour that has held since
2026-08-24, so it changes no source. (3) A third asserts that every result with `ok: false`,
including a contract-validation refusal on a done-when holding a literal placeholder, carries a
non-null `error` and a `detail`. Wherever a refusal is returned beside a live pointer, the
negative control is executed and logged at the base revision. Write paths:
`reckon/crew/dispatch.py` and that test.

## 6. §3 — sentences with two or more readings

R45. "The supervisor takes the snapshot after dispatch's writes and before it spawns the
worker, **then reports the launch**." against "reports the launch back to dispatch, **which
then prints its result and exits**" — (a) the report follows the scan and the spawn, so
dispatch's exit waits on the scan: the multi-minute hold is relocated, not removed, and §4's
"a dispatch is an ordinary short command" is false on reckon (244 worktrees × 3.3 s) even though
"Dispatch never runs a scan" is literally true; (b) the report is sent as soon as the worker is
spawned and dispatch's exit is not gated on the scan — but then part of the scan must follow
the spawn, contradicting "before it spawns the worker" (or the report must precede the scan,
contradicting "then"). Under (a) case 1's five-second bound cannot hold on any repository whose
scan is slow, which is the section's own measured defect; under (b) the section breaks the
baseline race constraint. The revision closed R8's *choice* but did not say whether the
supervisor's report may be sent before the snapshot completes. **material**

R46. "The first case runs against a temporary repository holding at least 30 registered
worktrees, so a scan that grows with the worktree count is visible in the time." — (a) 30
worktrees plus the five-second bound is enough to expose a scan on the return path; (b) it is
not: in a temporary repository each `git status` costs milliseconds, so 30 of them sit far
under five seconds, and the plan gives no per-tree cost for the fixture (the 3.3 s figure is
GPFS on a full checkworktree). Under (b) the fixture accepts an implementation that still scans
in dispatch, and the negative control ("putting the tree snapshot back … makes the first case
fail on the five-second bound") does not fail either — the control is asserted, not
established. **material**

R47. "The supervisor updates the pointer only by **atomic rename over an existing file. It
never creates one**, so a discarded pointer stays discarded." — (a) the rule binds: no write
may bring a discarded pointer back, so the implementer must use a discipline that cannot
create (or must own the residual race); (b) the mechanism named is taken as sufficient, but
`rename(2)`/`os.replace` creates its target: a rename a microsecond after a discard recreates
exactly what the sentence forbids, and it is the race the 2026-09-23 late-writer incident is
made of. Under (b) the pinned mechanism is the defect the pin exists to prevent. **material**

R48. "A worker that exits before dispatch returns is still `ok: true`, and its exit record
reads `ended_during: launch`." — (a) an illustration of the classification rule
(`stream_records_seen == 0` ⇒ `launch`); (b) an obligation keyed on a dispatch-side clock, so a
worker that emits a record before dispatch's return would be misclassified `working` under the
rule but the sentence says `launch`. The clause also reads as if the recorder knows when
dispatch returned, which it does not. **minor**

R49. Case 1: "the dispatch process exits within five seconds with the run id in its JSON
output, nothing is killed, and the stub's marker file appears" — R19's residue, unaddressed by
the revision: "the stub's marker file appears" carries no bound while the plan is explicit
elsewhere (five seconds, ten seconds) and the merged probe gives its own stub 90 s; "nothing is
killed" does not say on the host or for this run; and "the run id in its JSON output" still does
not name `ok: true` even though the pin bullet now defines the result — (a) the assertion is
satisfied by any JSON carrying a run id, however the run ended; (b) it is the pinned result,
id plus `ok: true`. **minor**

R50. Case 2: "SIGKILL **sent to the dispatch process before it returns** still leaves the marker
file appearing." — R20's target is now pinned (pid alone), but the case does not require the
signal to be witnessed landing while the dispatch was still running: the fixed dispatch is
required elsewhere to be near-instant (case 1's five-second bound), so a kill fired after its
exit is indistinguishable from one that arrived before the return, and the case passes on both
— a vacuous pass is available to an implementer of either reading. **minor**

R51. "the pointer's `repository_tree_snapshot` carries `{"available": false, "detail": ...}`" —
(a) the failure object is a wrapper *around* the existing base shape: success keeps
`{"version": 1, "status_digest": ..., "trees": [...]}` as `_repository_tree_snapshot` returns
it now, and only failures gain `available`; (b) both cases are wrapped, so a reader can ask
`payload["available"]` on any pointer. The base function returns no `available` key at all, so
under (a) a consumer keying on it KeyErrors on success; the plan states only the failure case.
**minor**

R18 (**carried forward, unaddressed**). "The record is named `worker_exit` wherever it
outlives the run directory" and "A reader composes nothing at read time that this record
carries" — (a) a forward obligation on the other two plans it names (they read the fields); (b)
a constraint §3 must enforce now, on readers outside its write paths — and the general naming
rule drawn from one instance still leaves three names for one object (`exit.json`, pointer
`exit`, ledger `worker_exit`) with no mapping table, so a worker may rename the file under the
general rule and break §7's fixture path. **material (scope)**

R15 (**carried forward**). Case 3: "both the run directory's exit record and the pointer
**carry the exit status** with signal name `SIGKILL`" against the table's "`exit_code` … null
when a signal ended it" — (a) an assertion of `signal`/`signal_name` (with `exit_code: null`);
(b) an assertion of `exit_code == -9`. **material**

R21 (**carried forward**). "the measure from worker-death-is-recorded §5, **verbatim**: a real
dispatch, the dispatching process gone, SIGKILL, and the exit status still recorded" — (a)
"verbatim" promises text a worker can find in that plan, but §5 is a six-item list with no
single quotable measure; (b) it is a perception — the four-step sequence restated here. Also
"a real dispatch" contradicts "covers four cases **with the stub backend of §2**". **material**

R22 (**carried forward, minor**). "within ten seconds **both** the run directory's exit record
and the pointer carry the exit status" — (a) the bound covers both writes; (b) the bound covers
the run-directory record alone, the pointer whenever.

R23 (**partially closed**). "**putting the tree snapshot back** between the launch and
dispatch's return makes the first case fail on the five-second bound" — the mutation is now
named (R23's first half closed); the second half ("removing the supervisor's wait makes the
third case fail") might, under (a), fail case 1 first if the launch report travels through the
supervisor's wait; under (b) case 1 is untouched and only case 3 fails. The plan asserts the
latter. **minor**

R24 (**carried forward, minor**). "Both failing logs are kept **with the manifest**" — (a)
referenced by path in the manifest; (b) placed beside the manifest file.

R25 (**carried forward, minor–material**). "The real pointer directory under the user's config
home is asserted untouched." — (a) entry-set unchanged; (b) no file mtime changed. §2's own
repaired paragraph shows the instrument that was needed (capture at module load plus a
sentinel that makes the guard fail); §3's sentence still names no instrument **(b)** — the
first review's R25 stands as written.

## 7. §4 — sentences with two or more readings

R54. "A literal grep for "background" is not the measure, because **unrelated sentences such
as the scout note at `skills/reckon-ship/references/sprint-orchestration.md` use the word**." —
(a) the scout note is an incidental use, so it needs no change; (b) the scout note is
*sprint-orchestration.md:684*, "**Dispatch a prior-art scout in the background.** One read-only
investigate-role node, dispatched through `reckon crew dispatch` exactly like every other
node" — the instruction the first review's R29 found and the one whose file the revision
*added* to the write paths. Under (b) — the reading the tree supports — the clause misdescribes
the section's own target, and a §4 worker that believes it leaves line 684 standing and then
fails the done-when's reading of its own manual list. The clause and the write-path addition
stand in contradiction in the same paragraph. **material**

R55. "the watcher's transition stream for that home carries no row naming it." — R33's residue:
(a) the test arms a real watcher on the temporary home and reads its stream — the only reading
that can fail; (b) it reads whatever stream file exists (~empty, vacuous). The revision's
wording names a watcher but still neither an API nor a file, and a watcher is still not in the
declared write paths. **minor–material**

R30 (**carried forward, minor**). "says a CLI dispatch returns **once its worker runs**" — (a)
the row must say that; (b) "runs" is the same loose phrase as before the revision
(§3's is "the worker process is running and its live pointer is written"), so the launch-kind
row may claim less than §3 delivers. The revision added "and is run in the foreground" but did
not tighten "runs".

## 8. §5 — sentences with two or more readings

R56. "After `crew discard` of a launch-failed review, its worktree stayed on disk, and a
re-dispatch of the same node id was refused with `worktree path already exists`. The refusal is
correct, but **the leftover worktree is what a discard should not leave**." — (a) context: an
aside that motivates the worktree half of the assertion; (b) a defect §5 owns — and then the
declared write paths (`reckon/crew/dispatch.py` and the test) cannot carry the fix, which
belongs to the discard path. Under (b) a worker hits a scope wall it cannot fix. **material**

R31 (**carried forward, unaddressed**). "remove the pointer on refusal, **or** order the checks
so every refusal precedes the write" — (a) either remedy satisfies §5; (b) only the second is
generally available, because §6 establishes refusals that can only follow the launch (a
refusal there must become success instead), so choosing (a)'s route at a post-launch site
fights §6's rule. The revision did not reconcile the two remedies. **material**

R34 (**carried forward, minor**). "received a row at 20:41:11 … **four seconds after a
dispatch**" — (a) after the dispatch started; (b) after the refusal returned — the only timing
the section gives for the state the new test must reproduce.

R57. "If none leaves a trace at the base revision, the manifest says so with the list, and the
section closes with the test added and no source change." — (a) the dry-run is a legitimate
outcome: the plan's comment `c-20260923T0955-the-hold-is-the-tree-snapshot` names the snapshot
handler as §6's candidate cause, and my reading of that handler (`dispatch.py:4010–4022`)
shows it unlinks the pointer and removes the run directory in its `except`, so "none leaves a
trace" is the likely base outcome and no site is repaired; (b) the section still expects a fix
somewhere. The two readings differ on whether §5 lands source changes at all — the read-back is
the only instrument that can settle it. **minor–material (the section is honest about this; the
release should not promise a repair)**

## 9. §6 — sentences with two or more readings

R58. "Separately, **find why an explicit `--member` was replaced by an auto-derived member**,
and refuse rather than substitute when the named member cannot be bound." — the revision
records the second half as already true since 2026-08-24 (a named member is refused, never
substituted; only a falsy `member` derives one), so (a) the "find why" half is answered by
reading the binding code — nothing could have substituted; or (b) it remains a live question
the incident's cause lies elsewhere (the flag never reaching the binding function, the session
derivation, another call site), which test 2 cannot answer and whose repair is outside the
write paths. The text keeps the imperative without naming which. **material**

R37 (**narrowed**). "drives a dispatch **through the failing path** with a stub backend, and
asserts that the result either names the launched run with `ok: true` or leaves no pointer and
no process" — (a) "the failing path" is the snapshot-handler path the §6 paragraph names as
candidate cause — but §3 moves that handler out of dispatch, so the path exists at the base
revision (where the plan orders the reproduction) and may not at §3's head, and the plan does
not say what the test drives there; (b) it is any path that returns a refusal beside a live
pointer, the population R38's third test leaves open. For "no process": (i) no live worker for
the run (`record_process_alive` on the pointer's pid); (ii) literally no process at all —
unrealisable inside a test that must run. **minor–material**

R35 (**carried forward**). "**Either** the dispatch reports success naming the run it
launched, **or** it launches nothing." — (a) a third state — refusal returned with a pointer
written and no worker — is permitted when the two clauses are read as alternative *outcomes*
scoped to the launched/non-launched split; (b) the pair is exhaustive over refuse-vs-succeed,
leaving the pointer's fate to §5's rule. The new done-when test 1 (assert the disjunction on
the driven path) narrows the reading but does not settle refusal-plus-pointer on the
post-launch path. **minor–material**

R38 (**carried forward**). "The contract-validation refusal returns `ok: false` with
`error: null`, carrying its reason only under `validation.findings`." / "A missing `error` on a
contract-validation refusal is fixed either way." — (a) the claim describes one site; (b) it
describes every validation refusal. By reading at `12155ff8`: the live dispatch's validation
refusal uses `error: "not-dispatchable"` with a detail (`reckon/cli.py:1368–1371`), while the
**dry-run** dispatch emits `ok: resolution.validation.ok` with no `error` key at all and the
findings under `validation` (`reckon/cli.py:1267`, `as_dict` in `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:1781`)
— which is the shape the sentence must mean (absent key rather than null). The third test's
"**every** result with `ok: false`" does not say over what population (the paths the test
exercises, or every refusal the CLI emits), and "fixed either way" names `error` only while
the test requires `error` **and** `detail`. **minor–material**

R59. "so its worker was running" — (a) the worker was alive at the moment the run was checked;
(b) the worker had been alive — the offered proof is a stream written eleven seconds before
the check, which does not by itself distinguish a worker that had died eleven seconds earlier
from one still working. The section states the conclusion as established. **minor**

R44 (**cross-plan, carried**). §3's `ended_during: launch|working` and
worker-death-is-recorded §5's `interrupted` vocabulary both classify a death; neither plan
fixes the precedence. **material across plans**

# Third read-back: §3, §4, §6 and §9 of a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker, as revised at d27aef14

Scope: `docs/plans/a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker.html`, §3 (the prose and its pinned
readings), §4, §6 and §9, as revised at `d27aef14` ("dispatch returns before the supervisor's
snapshot, and the supervisor never writes the pointer"). Blob at that revision: `4e8de7ad`,
`plan-version 39`.

**Revision identity, checked.** `git diff d27aef14 HEAD -- <path>` is *not* empty today: the
plan has moved twice since the revision, and neither move touches the four sections' prose.

- `plan-version` 39 → 40 (server-written on a POST), and
- one comment added to the plan's comment section, `c-run-r-20260923T131057549188-second-review…`
  ("28 two-reading sentences reported; the five material ones were applied to the plan at
  d27aef14 and a third read-back follows").

So the text restated below is the text that would be released if §3, §4, §6 and §9 are released
from this revision. The cause of the drift is that the served plan is the working tree, and two
promotions have POSTed to it since d27aef14; a release should cite the revision and not "the
plan as it stands".

This is a read-back: every judgement below is by reading the text and the tree at this node's
base `6d019047`; no test was run and no repository path was changed. Reading ids continue the
prior report's numbering; new items start at R61.

## 1. §3 in my own words

**Intent.** Today a dispatch's shell is held open after its worker starts, by the post-launch
repository tree snapshot — one `git status --untracked-files=all` per registered worktree in
`_repository_tree_snapshot(repo_root)`, sequentially, measured at 244 × 3.3 s on reckon. §3
removes the hold by rebuilding the launch around a small detached per-run supervisor: dispatch
prints its JSON result and exits as soon as the supervisor process is running and the live
pointer naming it is written, and the supervisor does the rest. The worker must survive the
dispatch process ending by any of §2's three signals — SIGTERM, SIGKILL, SIGHUP — *including* a
signal arriving during what is today the hold.

The snapshot is a boundary baseline, so its placement is constrained both ways: it must follow
dispatch's own writes and predate any write the worker makes in another tree. The revision's
placement rule is that the **supervisor** takes it, after dispatch's writes and before it spawns
the worker, and that **dispatch waits for neither** — so the scan delays the worker's start and
never dispatch's return. Dispatch never runs, and never waits on, a scan that grows with the
worktree count.

**What moved off the pointer.** The supervisor writes the snapshot to the run directory's
`tree-snapshot.json`, and promotion's out-of-fence check — the reader of
`record.get("repository_tree_snapshot")` in `reckon/crew/promotion.py` — reads it from there,
falling back to the pointer field for runs dispatched before this change. A snapshot that raises
writes `{"available": false, "detail": ...}` to `tree-snapshot.json` and the supervisor still
spawns the worker: a snapshot failure never kills a worker and never turns a launch into a
refusal.

**The six pinned readings.**

1. *What dispatch's result means.* `ok: true` with the run id once the supervisor process is
   running and the pointer records the supervisor's pid and start time; it waits for neither the
   snapshot nor the worker's spawn. A worker that is never spawned, or that exits before its
   first stream record, is still `ok: true` and its exit record reads `ended_during: launch`.
   `ok: false` means no supervisor was started.
2. *The pointer's pid is the supervisor's.* The supervisor lives from dispatch until it has
   recorded the worker's exit, so it is alive exactly while the supervisor is alive, and
   `record_process_alive` keeps reading the pointer's pid unchanged. The worker's pid goes to the
   run directory's `worker.json` at spawn.
3. *The SIGKILL case and `crew stop`.* A signal to the dispatch process goes to its pid alone,
   never its process group — which is how a host expiring a background shell ends it. The
   supervisor starts its own session and process group and spawns the worker inside that group,
   not in a further session; `terminate` in `reckon/crew/dispatch.py` already signals the process
   group of the pointer's pid, so `crew stop` reaches the worker unchanged. The supervisor
   ignores SIGTERM and SIGHUP so it survives to collect the exit the stop caused.
4. *The supervisor never writes the pointer.* Dispatch creates the pointer; only dispatch,
   discard and promotion write it. Everything the supervisor produces (`tree-snapshot.json`,
   `worker.json`, `exit.json`) goes into the run directory, and the supervisor never creates that
   directory: it opens each file inside it and, when the directory is gone, drops the write. So
   neither a late write nor a rename can bring a discarded run back.
5. *The stream count.* `stream_records_seen` counts records across every path
   `stream_paths_newest_first` in `reckon/crew/recovery.py` returns for the run directory;
   `ended_during` is one of the record's fields.
6. *§1 and §2 are superseded where they conflict.* §1's "not established" and §2's three
   candidate holders predate the measurement; the holder is the tree snapshot. §2's probe
   (`tests/test_dispatch_lifetime_probe.py`) is merged and opt-in
   (`RECKON_RUN_DISPATCH_PROBE=1`) and measured that the worker survives SIGTERM, SIGKILL and
   SIGHUP to the dispatch process's pid — which fits §1's `timeout` anecdote, because `timeout`
   signals the process group.

**Exit collection moves to the supervisor, never to init.** A worker reparented to init has its
exit collected by init and reckon never sees it (0 exit records across every live pointer after a
night of at least eight deaths). Dispatch starts the supervisor in its own session, writes the
pointer naming it, prints its result and exits; the supervisor takes the snapshot, spawns the
worker, writes `worker.json`, waits on the worker, records the exit status and — when a signal
ended it — the signal name, writes that record to the run directory, and exits. It never writes
the pointer, because a discarded pointer was rewritten back into existence by a late writer, and
the run-directory record is what survives a discard.

**One record serves a death and a launch failure.** `exit.json` in the run directory, and now the
only copy (the pointer's `exit` key copy is gone from this revision). Nine fields —
`worker_pid`, `launched_at`, `exited_at`, `exit_code`, `signal`, `signal_name`,
`stream_records_seen`, `last_record_type`, `recorded_by` — plus `ended_during`, one of `launch`
or `working`, read as `launch` exactly when `stream_records_seen == 0`. A reader composes nothing
at read time that the record carries; a launch failure is this record with `ended_during: launch`,
not a second format. Wherever the record outlives the run directory it is named `worker_exit`,
because `exit_status` already holds the gate command's status (2,721 ledger rows carry it) and a
worker's exit under the same key would be two quantities in one field; carrying it into the
ledger row is §7.

**Done-when, in my own words.** `tests/test_dispatch_returns_without_worker.py`, four cases, the
§2 stub backend, a temporary repository and a temporary `RECKON_HOME`.

1. The dispatch process exits within five seconds with the run id in its JSON output, nothing is
   killed, and the stub's marker file appears. This case makes a scan slow on purpose: at least
   30 registered worktrees, and a `git` shim first on `PATH` that sleeps one second before
   delegating any `status` call to the real `git`, so a scan costs at least 30 s and cannot hide
   under the five-second bound. The same case asserts `tree-snapshot.json` appears in the case's
   run directory after the scan and that the promotion check reads it.
2. SIGKILL sent to the dispatch process before it returns still leaves the marker file appearing.
3. The worker-death-is-recorded §5 measure verbatim: a real dispatch, the dispatching process
   gone, SIGKILL, and the exit status still recorded — realised as dispatch returned, the worker
   SIGKILLed, and within ten seconds the run directory's `exit.json` carries the exit status with
   signal name `SIGKILL`.
4. The pointer is discarded **and the run directory removed** before the worker exits, and after
   the exit neither a pointer nor a run directory exists for the run.

Negative control executed and logged: putting the tree snapshot back between the launch and
dispatch's return makes case 1 fail on the five-second bound, and removing the supervisor's wait
makes case 3 fail; both failing logs are kept with the manifest. The real pointer directory under
the user's config home is asserted untouched. Write paths: `reckon/crew/dispatch.py`,
`reckon/crew/promotion.py`, `tests/test_dispatch_returns_without_worker.py` (the second file is
this revision's addition, for the promotion-side reader).

## 2. §4 in my own words

**Intent.** Once §3 lands, a dispatch is an ordinary short command, so no instruction may tell a
coordinator to background a CLI dispatch or to yield after it: the coordinator runs it in the
foreground and reads its JSON. The follower remains the only long-lived process a session
*watches*; §3's per-run supervisors are detached and belong to their runs, so a session neither
watches nor waits on them.

**Done-when, in my own words.** The launch-kind table in `skills/reckon-ship/SKILL.md` says a CLI
dispatch returns once its supervisor is running, before the worker is spawned, and is run in the
foreground. `skills/reckon-ship/references/orchestrator-harness/claude-code.md` names the
follower as the session's only monitor. No sentence in `skills/` instructs backgrounding a
`reckon crew dispatch`, checked by reading `grep -rn "dispatch" skills/` line by line, with the
lines that mention backgrounding listed in the manifest. Two such sentences are known and are
**targets, not incidental uses**: the scout note in
`skills/reckon-ship/references/sprint-orchestration.md` ("Dispatch a prior-art scout in the
background") and the line in `skills/reckon-ship/references/worker-backends.md` that backgrounds
a CLI dispatch and yields. A scout is still dispatched first and not waited on, because the
coordinator reads its result later, but the dispatch command itself runs in the foreground. Write
paths: the two files above plus `sprint-orchestration.md` and `worker-backends.md`; a
backgrounding sentence in any other file is a manifest follow-on. The section runs after §3
merges.

## 3. §6 in my own words

**Intent.** imas-efit-e3 measured, at about 02:58 UTC in `ship-s13-20260922`, two consecutive
refusals of one node (`not-dispatchable`, then `dispatch-refused` because the worktree path
already existed) while the run's pointer was live, an attempt had started at 02:58:27, and its
stream was eleven seconds old when checked — so its worker was running. Its member was an
auto-derived session member although both calls passed `--member clive-rev-6`. Such a refusal
invites two harmful moves: a duplicate dispatch of a node already working, and the removal of a
live process's worktree.

**The rule.** Find the path on which a refusal is returned after the pointer is written or the
worker is spawned, and make the result agree with what happened: either the dispatch reports
success naming the run it launched, or it launches nothing. The member question is now answered
**from records rather than from code**: a named `--member` has been refused rather than
substituted since 2026-08-24, so the node reads which dispatch launched the live run — the
pointer's `member`, and the argv and prompt in its run directory. If that launch passed no
`--member`, the substitution is refuted and the manifest says so; if it passed one, the manifest
names the call path that dropped it, repaired when it lies in the write paths and listed as a
follow-on otherwise.

**The exit status is not the defect.** imas-efit-e3 first reported that refusals exited 0, then
measured the bare command and corrected it: `dispatch-refused` exits 1, a contract-validation
refusal exits 2, and every 0 ended in a pipe, which reports the last stage's status; a refused
dry run measured here also exits 1. One part of that correction *is* a defect: the
contract-validation refusal returns `ok: false` with `error: null`, carrying its reason only under
`validation.findings`, while the skill documents that every refusal carries `error` and `detail`,
so a caller keying on `error` reads that refusal as success — imas-efit-e3 did.

**Done-when, in my own words.** `tests/test_dispatch_result_matches_launch.py`:

1. Drives a dispatch with a stub backend in a temporary crew config home through the path the
   reproduction finds returning a refusal beside a live pointer, and drives the same inputs at
   the node's head whether or not §3 has removed that path; asserts the result either names the
   launched run with `ok: true` or leaves no pointer and no worker that `record_process_alive`
   reports alive.
2. A second test passes an unresolvable `--member` and asserts a refusal with no auto-derived
   member bound. The read-back found this behaviour has held since 2026-08-24, so the test
   verifies existing behaviour and changes no source.
3. A third covers **every** `ok: false` result the `reckon crew dispatch` command emits, live and
   `--dry-run` alike, including a contract-validation refusal on a done-when holding a literal
   placeholder; asserts each carries `error` and `detail` as non-empty strings, so an absent key
   fails as a null one does.

The reproduction comes first: the candidate cause is the exception handler around the post-launch
tree snapshot, which signals the launched worker's process group and returns a refusal; §3 moves
that snapshot into the supervisor, so the node reproduces at the base revision and states whether
§3's change already removes the path. The negative control is executed and logged at the base
revision wherever a refusal is returned beside a live pointer. A missing `error` or `detail` on
any refusal is fixed either way. The dry-run and live validation refusals are composed in
`reckon/cli.py` (the dispatch command's dry-run verdict and its `not-dispatchable` branch). Write
paths: `reckon/crew/dispatch.py`, `reckon/cli.py` (corrected: `reckon/cli.py`) and that test.

## 4. §9 in my own words

**Intent.** Measured in this sprint: after `crew discard` of a launch-failed review, its worktree
stayed on disk and a re-dispatch of the same node id was refused `worktree path already exists`.
The refusal is correct. `discard` in `reckon/crew/promotion.py` unlinks only the pointer and
leaves the worktree to `crew gc`, so a node whose launch failed cannot be retried under its own id
until a collection runs; the coordinator then mints a second id and the ledger reads two nodes
where there was one.

**The rule.** Discard removes the run's worktree when, and only when, it holds no work: its
`status --porcelain` (corrected: `--porcelain`) is empty apart from the provisioned `.venv` and
`.env` links, and its `HEAD` equals the pointer's `base_sha`. Otherwise it keeps the worktree and
says why in its result, under `worktree_kept`. A worktree with a commit or an uncommitted change
is never removed by a discard, and no removal is ever forced.

**Done-when, in my own words.** `tests/test_discard_removes_an_empty_worktree.py`, temporary
repository and `RECKON_HOME`, discards three stopped runs. The first worktree is clean at its
base: it is removed, and a dispatch of the same node id then succeeds. The second holds a commit
and the third an uncommitted file: both are kept, and the result names each reason. The negative
control is executed and logged — removing the clean-and-at-base check makes the second and third
worktrees disappear and the test fail. Runs after §3, which also writes
`reckon/crew/promotion.py`. Write paths: `reckon/crew/promotion.py` and that test.

| R56 | §5 → §9 | **Closed.** The discard case is now §9, with its own write paths |
| R57 | §5 (landed) | **Closed by the landing.** The dry-run was not the outcome: of six refusal paths, four were clean and two were not, and both were repaired |
| R58 | §6 | **Closed as a reading.** The imperative is replaced by a records-based procedure with a stated manifest outcome for each branch |
| R59 | §6 | **Open, unchanged.** "so its worker was running" still reads as established from a stream eleven seconds old |
| R60 | §3 | **Open, unchanged.** The stream-count bullet still does not mention the `include=` argument; verified present in the tree (see §7) |

**Note A — R44 is answered in a comment, not in a section.** The revision comment
`c-20260923T1515-second-readback-applied` states the precedence: "when `exit.json` exists it is
the collected fact and wins; worker-death-is-recorded's `interrupted` label applies only to a run
with no exit record." The four sections do not carry it. `grep -c interrupted` over §3's lines is
**0**, over §6's is 0, and no sentence in §3 states the precedence between `ended_during` and
`interrupted`. The other plan does define `interrupted` (worker-death-is-recorded §3's vocabulary
table and §4's rule) and does not state the precedence either. So a cross-plan item that this
plan's comment claims to resolve is not in any released section text — the comment is served with
the plan, which is a real mitigation, but a section release should carry the rule that governs
§3's own vocabulary.

### Carried items that sit in these four sections

- **R15 (§3 case 3)** — open, in reduced form. "carries the exit status with signal name
  `SIGKILL`" still admits (a) `signal`/`signal_name` with `exit_code: null` or (b)
  `exit_code == -9`, against a table that says `exit_code` is null for a signal. The revision
  removed the pointer's copy, so only one writer is now in question, but the assertion's content
  is as it was.
- **R18 (§3)** — closed. The pointer copy is gone, so the object has two names (file `exit.json`,
  ledger key `worker_exit`) and both are defined where they are used, not three names with no map.
- **R21 (§3 case 3)** — open. "verbatim" still promises a quotable measure from a section that is
  a list, and "a real dispatch" still sits against the case list's "with the stub backend of §2".
- **R22 (§3 case 4)** — closed. The sentence now names one writer and one bound.
- **R23 (§3 negative control, second half)** — closed by the R45 fix, text unchanged: the launch
  report no longer travels through the supervisor's wait, so case 1 is untouched when the wait is
  removed.
- **R24 (§3)** — open, minor. "kept with the manifest" still does not say by path or beside it.
- **R25 (§3)** — open. "The real pointer directory under the user's config home is asserted
  untouched" still names no instrument; §5's landed test shows the one that works (a positive
  control that makes the read see something present).
- **R30 (§4)** — closed as to "runs" ("once its supervisor is running, before the worker is
  spawned"), but the tightening introduces a claim §3 does not guarantee — see **R65.
- **R31 (§5)** — out of these four sections; §5 landed, so the "either remedy" question is moot
  at the site it arose.
- **R34 (§5)** — out of these four sections.
- **R35 (§6)** — open, and now the sharper one. The done-when asserts the disjunction on the
  driven path, but a refusal returned *for a dispatch that launched a worker* can satisfy "leaves
  no pointer and no worker that `record_process_alive` reports alive" whenever the refusal killed
  the worker and removed the pointer — which is exactly the §5-handler shape §6 names as its
  candidate cause. For a launched dispatch the run *did* launch; the assertion cannot distinguish
  it from one that never launched.
- **R37 (§6)** — narrowed, residual stands: "no process" is fixed by naming
  `record_process_alive`, and the path to drive is now "the path the reproduction finds", which
  is the records-based reproduction rather than "the failing path" — but see R66 on which
  revision's base that reproduction still holds at.
- **R38 (§6)** — narrowed: `reckon/cli.py` is now a write path, so the absent-key shape is
  fixable, and "every `ok: false` the command emits" is stated over an explicit population (live
  and dry-run). The "either way" sentence now names `error` **and** `detail`, matching the test.

## 6. Sentences with two or more readings

New items R61–R69. Sentences that survive from the prior report with the same readings are not
repeated here; they are named in §5.

**R61 (§3, done-when case 1).** "the dispatch process exits within five seconds with the run id
in its JSON output, nothing is killed, and the stub's marker file appears" — the revision makes
the marker's arrival *provably later than five seconds* (the scan costs ≥30 s by construction and
the worker is spawned after it), so (a) all three assertions sit inside the five-second window is
now impossible; (b) the bound covers the dispatch's exit alone, and "the marker file appears" is
asserted at some later, unstated time. Only (b) can be meant; the section does not say it, and (a)
is what the sentence says. R49's "carries no bound" residue is now the whole of the item, and the
case cannot be written by an implementer without choosing. **material**

**R62 (§3, done-when case 1).** "the test puts a `git` shim first on `PATH` that sleeps one
second before delegating any `status` call to the real `git`" — (a) every `git status` in the
launch path pays the second, so the case measures the sum of all status calls, not the scan's
placement: if any of dispatch's own pre-return steps call `git status`, the shim charges them
too, and the five-second bound then tests the sum; (b) the shim is meant to charge the scan alone
and the rest of the path is known to make no status call. The section does not say which, and the
two differ in what the negative control proves. **minor**

**R63 (§3, prose against case 4).** "The run-directory record is what survives a discard" against
case 4's "the pointer is discarded **and the run directory removed** before the worker exits". —
(a) the prose describes the pointer-only discard of today, and case 4 escalates it, so the
record's survival is never asserted by any case; (b) case 4 is *the* discard and the prose's claim
is false under it. Reading (a) is almost certainly meant; the item is that the survival claim
carries no case, and the case carries no survival assertion. **minor–material**

**R64 (§3, the snapshot-ordering sentence).** "The supervisor takes the snapshot after dispatch's
own writes and before it spawns the worker." — (a) an invariant the implementation must hold, so
the supervisor cannot be allowed to snapshot until dispatch's writes are complete; (b) a
description of intent, holding because dispatch's tree-touching writes happen to precede the
supervisor's spawn. Under §3's own "dispatch waits for neither", the supervisor is running while
dispatch makes its last writes, and the section names no handshake between them — while §5's
landed record shows one dispatch write (the session-member registration) that *does* touch the
repository, which is the reason the code's comment takes the baseline last today. **material**

**R65 (§4, done-when).** "says a CLI dispatch returns once its supervisor is running, **before
the worker is spawned**" — (a) an ordering guarantee the skill row must state, which §3 does not
deliver: §3 promises that dispatch waits for neither the snapshot nor the row, not that the
spawn follows dispatch's exit, and the supervisor may spawn during dispatch's exit; (b) a
statement of what dispatch does not wait for. Under (a) the §4 node must either weaken the row or
§3 must add the ordering. **material**

**R66 (§6, the reproduction's base).** "§3 moves that snapshot into the supervisor, so the node
reproduces against the base revision and states whether §3's change already removes the path."
— (a) the base revision is the node's own base, where the node re-establishes the reproduction
before repairing; (b) the base is the one the recorded reproduction came from. These are not the
same: §5 landed at `4248cbaf` (before this revision) and repaired the unwind so that the
remover's refusal on the run's own claim no longer aborts the unwind — and that refusal is the
mechanism the plan's own reproduction comment `c-20260923T1335-s6-reproduced` records, verbatim
("refusing to remove worktree … claimed by live runs <its own run>"). §6's paragraph names the
snapshot *call* as the candidate cause while the comment names the *cleanup* refusal as the
observed mechanism, and the two are different code. A §6 node that reproduces at its own base may
find nothing to reproduce, and the plan does not tell it which reading governs. **material**

**R67 (§9, against the tree).** "A worktree with a commit or an uncommitted change is never
removed by a discard, and no removal is ever forced." against the section's own negative control,
"removing the clean-and-at-base check makes the second and third worktrees disappear" — the
existing remover is `_remove_worktree` in `reckon/crew/routing.py`, which runs
`git worktree remove --force` unconditionally (line 917). So (a) "no removal is ever forced"
means no removal proceeds without the emptiness check, and the control works because the git call
keeps `--force`; (b) the git invocation must drop the flag, and then the control's prediction
depends on git refusing a tree carrying the provisioned `.venv`/`.env` links. Only (a) makes the
control's outcome deterministic, and only (a) reconciles with the sentence's own words. **minor**

**R68 (§9, "holds no work").** "removes the run's worktree when, and only when, it holds no
work: its `status --porcelain` is empty apart from the provisioned `.venv` and `.env` links, and
its `HEAD` equals the pointer's `base_sha`." — (a) the two conditions *define* "holds no work",
so a worktree whose only content is a gitignored artifact (a figure, a log, a scratch output a
worker left, or any LIFO/temp file under an ignored path) is "empty" and is removed with its
content; (b) "holds no work" governs and the conditions are its test, so an ignored artifact is
work kept. **minor**

**R69 (§9, discarded runs).** "discards three stopped runs" — (a) the three pointers are
discardable because their recorded process is not alive, which is `discard`'s only guard
(`record_process_alive(...) is True` raises); (b) "stopped" is a state of the run and may mean
terminal. A terminal run has no pointer and cannot be discarded, so only (a) yields three
discards. **minor** (verified against the tree, §7.)

## 7. Named referents checked against the tree (read-only, at 6d019047)

- `_repository_tree_snapshot` is at `reckon/crew/routing.py:387`; the call the plan describes is
  `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:4021`, assigning `record["repository_tree_snapshot"]`, taken under the
  comment "Registration can update the repository's committed crew ledger. Take the boundary
  baseline only after dispatch's own writes are complete" — which is the write R64 turns on.
- Promotion's reader exists: `reckon/crew/promotion.py:1244 `snapshot =
  record.get("repository_tree_snapshot")`, so §3's claim about the fallback has a real site.
- `discard` exists at `reckon/crew/promotion.py:4147`, and §9's premise is exact: it refuses a
  live run (`cannot discard live run {run_id!r}: recorded pid {pid} is alive`) and then does
  `pointer_path(run_id).unlink()` — nothing else. Its returned dict has no `worktree_kept` key,
  which §9 adds.
- The pointer carries `base_sha` (written at `dispatch.py:2848`, `3077`, `3263`, `3841`), so
  §9's second condition has a field to read.
- `_remove_worktree` is `reckon/crew/routing.py:908`; it refuses a worktree with live claims and
  then runs `git worktree remove --force` (line 917) — R67.
- `terminate` is `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:5909`; it signals the **process group** of the pointer's
  pid (`_signal_process_group(...)`) and records "SIGTERM sent to process group of pid N" — §3's
  `crew stop` claim holds against the tree.
- The current post-launch handler (`dispatch.py:~4017-4044`) wraps the pointer write, peer wiring,
  watcher read, member registration and the snapshot, signals the launched worker's group on
  exception, then unlinks the pointer, removes the run directory and removes the worktree, and
  re-raises — the shape §6 names, after §5's repair of the unwind order.
- `stream_paths_newest_first` is `reckon/crew/recovery.py:2056` in the prior report and
  `recovery.py:2263` today; its first line of documentation for the `include=` argument is "The
  pointer's own log path joins the candidates when a caller supplies it, so a record whose
  current stream sits outside the run directory is still read" — R60's residue is real, and the
  line number the prior report gave has already rotted, which is the plan's own rule about
  citations by function rather than by line.
- `tests/test_refused_dispatch_leaves_no_pointer.py` exists (14,661 bytes) with six tests,
  including `test_a_launched_dispatch_is_seen_by_every_read` (the positive control R55 wanted) and
  a producer-driven watcher read that appends transitions exactly as the watcher seat does.

§6 — A refusal is never returned for a dispatch that launched

unknown · undated

One refusal-beside-a-launch mechanism is removed at merge 1859c0d19. _confirm_supervisor_survived refused a dispatch whose supervisor had already written a clean completion record for the worker it spawned: a fast two-tree launch finishes inside the two-second survival window. That is this section's defect, a refusal returned for a dispatch that launched. The check now accepts a record naming the spawned worker and returns the run. A supervisor that exits in the window with no such record is still refused. Test test_dispatch_returns_when_its_supervisor_finished_the_launch in tests/test_dispatch_returns_without_worker.py covers it, and its negative control (the acceptance removed) is red. Its review found one weakness to repair: the case asserts only that dispatch returned, so a launch slower than the window would pass on the deadline, not the acceptance. A follow-on node pins the return to the record. This section's own test file, tests/test_dispatch_result_matches_launch.py, should drive this mechanism among the others it reproduces.

Landed at merge b5e86a772 (run r-20261001T081548169030-a-dispatch-result-matches-its-launch, reviewed head 634d7d3cd). Every ok:false the dispatch command emits now carries error and detail. §7 — A promoted run keeps its worker's exit

reckon-build · 2026-09-24T20:30:00Z

§7 landed (2a33d2db). complete in reckon/crew/promotion.py reads the run directory's exit.json once, before the run directory is released, and copies it verbatim onto the promoted row under worker_exit. The row's gate_check.exit_status is a separate fact: the worker's exit never writes it. A run with no exit.json carries no worker_exit key at all, because the key is written only when a record was read — a present-but-empty object would read as a supervisor that ran and recorded nothing. A file that exists but cannot be read, or does not parse as a JSON object, is treated as absent for the same reason: propagation would put a failure about the record where the record should be. Evidence: two cases in tests/test_promotion_keeps_the_worker_exit.py, both passing, and both declared negative controls reproduce as written — removing the copy fails case 1, and writing an empty key when the file is absent fails case 2. The promotion-import file set shows zero added failures against base 02e2757d: an identical 30-failure set, 246 passed, at both revisions. Landing record and figure in §7 of the landed evidence record.

reckon-build s21-coord · 2026-09-24T21:53:00Z

Gate passed and merged at 13c2cf55 (commits 2a33d2db, be77daad). tests/test_promotion_keeps_the_worker_exit.py: 2 passed, and both declared negative controls were executed, each failing only the case it names. The promotion-importing files show the same 30 pre-existing failures at base 02e2757d and at head. Review 95. One finding is still open: the new docstring (promotion.py ~601), the evidence anchor and the landing comment above say the run directory is released at promotion, but promotion releases only the pointer and the worktree, and the run directory lives until crew gc (promotion.py ~636). A wording correction is queued as its own node after the gate-log-digest fix, which writes the same file. §7 is closed; the executable denominator is §2–§10 without §1, which is 9 sections with 4 done.

a promoted row carries exit.json verbatim under worker_exit, no key when absent; both negative controls fail as declared; promotion importers same 30 failures at base and head; review 95, docstring overstates run-dir release

reckon-build · 2026-09-24T21:53:00.901Z

reckon-build · 2026-09-25T00:56:20.293Z

review of the-worker-exit-docstring fix stored at 86; two more overstating comments in promotion.py and one in the test docstring

reckon-build · 2026-09-25T00:00:00Z

Wording correction (5e7515e8). The _promoted_worker_exit docstring in reckon/crew/promotion.py no longer says the run directory is released at promotion or that the ledger row is the only record that outlives the run. Promotion releases the run's live pointer and its worktree; the run directory survives, exit.json with it, until crew gc prunes it on a retention window, so the row is the durable copy once gc has run — the same fact _preserve_cited_gate_log's docstring already states. The overstatement in the §7 evidence record was corrected to match. Text only: no code line changed. Checks: a grep of reckon/crew/promotion.py for released at promotion and only record that outlives returns nothing (exit 1) with a positive control in the same log, so the empty result is the file's rather than the instrument's; tests/test_promotion_keeps_the_worker_exit.py 2 passed. Logs grep.log and test.log in the run directory. This closes the review finding above; the previous landing comment's phrase "before the run directory is released" is superseded here rather than rewritten, so the log still shows what was told. A follow-up text pass (1a494b54) found three further comments in the same module with the same overstatement in different wording (the routing-evidence, worker-exit and shadow-contamination comments) and corrected all three; the module now carries no statement that promotion releases or deletes the run directory.

reckon-build s21-coord · 2026-09-25T01:30:00Z

A finding for the supervisor's session verb, relayed from imas-ambix-a8 through reckon-ee on 2026-09-25, and to settle before reckon.crew.fleet_supervisor becomes job 1277272's batch step. On zellij 0.45.1, tabs opened in a session before any client attaches have no usable size: the server logs 'Failed to apply layout: Not enough room for panes' for each tab. The next client to attach then panics the server ('failed to attach client 2 to tab with index 3', followed by router and wasm panics), and the client sees 'Error occurred in server:'. The panic emits no stall line. The supervisor's session verb, ported from the dotfiles reference, creates sessions headless with `zellij --layout -fleet attach --create-background --create NAME`, so any multi-tab fleet layout is exposed. The same four tabs opened with a client already attached are clean. Verbatim excerpt: imas-ambix docs/figures/fleet-view-rendering/evidence-second-client/4tab-pre-server-panic.txt at dacdbbfd. Live fleet servers are unaffected. Before the batch-step cutover, the session verb either creates a single-tab session and opens further tabs only once a client attaches, or passes an explicit size. A test starting a multi-tab layout headless and then attaching must show no server panic. Related measurement: a Claude Code pane takes 3.76 s to reflow on a 100→70 column resize, against 13 ms for a shell pane.

reckon-build s21-coord · 2026-09-25T06:25:00Z

Lead decisions, 2026-09-25. (1) The headless multi-tab panic is fixed without restricting tabs. zellij 0.45.1 is the latest release (published 2026-08-28), so an upgrade cannot fix it. It is the open upstream regression zellij-org/zellij#5594, caused by per-client tab sizes in 0.45.0; the fix PR #5612 is unmerged, and 0.44.3 is unaffected. The supervisor's session verb adopts the workaround recorded on that issue. After creating a session with attach --create-background and its layout, it attaches a short-lived client on a pty of fixed size (for example 200x50) while the layout's tabs are created, then detaches, so every tab survives the first real attach. The workaround is removed once a release carries #5612. The verb's test drives a stub zellij and asserts the sized client attaches before the tabs are made and detaches after. An on-node check against the real 0.45.1 binary creates a throwaway four-tab session headless, attaches a second client, asserts no server panic line in the session log, then deletes only that session by its own name. (2) The on-node scancel proof runs right after the batch-step cutover, as a throwaway: a dedicated test session step, one trivial worker dispatched from it, then scancel of that step id alone, recording the surviving worker's parent. The cutover itself stays a lead-approved step on the live allocation.

reckon-build s21-coord · 2026-09-25T06:30:00Z

A finding from imas-ambix-a8, relayed by reckon-ee on 2026-09-25, queued as the next supervisor node after headless-tabs-survive-attach, which writes the same file. Every fleet pane inherited the tool-shell variables of the Claude Code session that submitted the job: AI_AGENT, GIT_EDITOR=true, COREPACK_ENABLE_AUTO_PIN=0 and NoDefaultCurrentDirectoryInExePath=1, plus SLURM's ENVIRONMENT=BATCH. The supervisor strips only CLAUDE*, ZELLIJ* and CX_* names. With GIT_EDITOR=true, a `git commit` without -m in a fleet pane silently takes the default message. The dotfiles supervisor now unsets all five (dotfiles 6a375a1). reckon.crew.fleet_supervisor builds a pane's and a spawned child's environment from an allow-list of the variables a session needs (HOME, USER, LOGNAME, PATH, SHELL, TERM, LANG, LC_*, TMPDIR, XDG_*, SLURM_JOB_ID and the step variables a worker reads), not from a deny-list, so the next tool-shell variable cannot leak the same way. The test asserts a planted unknown variable is absent from a spawned child and every allow-listed one present, and a negative control reverts to the deny-list.

The batch step has no RECKON_HOME; the supervisor carries the scratch one, taken from the spec. scancel 1277272.1021 at 16:41:52Z ended the dispatcher (2077546). The supervisor, bwrap and worker stayed alive with the same parents.
  • Negative control: step 1277272.1020, flag unset. The dispatcher (2073383), the forked supervisor (2073694), bwrap (2073784) and the worker (2073785) were all in step_1020. scancel 1277272.1020 at 16:41:29Z killed all four.

Cleanup. The stub worker was stopped by pid. The supervisor recorded the exit (exit_code 143, recorded_by supervisor) and exited. It stays a zombie of the batch step until the next FIFO request, because serve() reaps finished children between requests. That delay is bounded and by design. The declared-services thread tolerates a collection made elsewhere by falling back to its lock. The node-local scratch was removed, and no pointer was written to the real crew home.

Context. Since this morning, sessions on 1277272 live inside step_batch under the zellij servers, so their forked supervisors are already in the batch step. The fleet lane is what protects a dispatcher running in any other step, and that is the case exercised here. From imas-ambix's side, §10 can be declared done, which opens gate g-tllitd-8. Declaring it and moving impl are left to the S21 coordinator.

# Reuse Map — seven S21 nodes

**Node:** `s21-reuse-map` · **Role:** investigate · **Plan:** `reckon:a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker §10` · **Tree:** `/home/ITER/mcintos/Code/.reckon-worktrees/reckon-c8f839407e49/s21-coord/s21-reuse-map` @ `318be1e5` (reckon main head at dispatch) · **Date:** 2026-09-24

Each node below is one dispatchable plan section. The reuse column names the machinery that already exists and that the node must compose from rather than rebuild; the write-path column is the plan's own "The write paths are …" clause where the section has one, and is marked **derived** where the plan names none. A citation "resolves at HEAD" when `grep -n '<symbol>' <path>` at `318be1e5` prints the cited line; the verification pass that checked every row is recorded at the end of this file with its tally.

## How the seven nodes were read

The measure names them by plan and section. Read at HEAD, the sections are:

| # | Plan | § | Section title at HEAD | Status at HEAD |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `the-page-paints-from-an-index` | 2 | The server listens before it watches | implementable |
| 2 | `a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker` | 4 | The skill stops telling coordinators to background a dispatch | implementable |
| 3 | `a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker` | 7 | A promoted run keeps its worker's exit | implementable |
| 4 | `a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker` | 10 | A worker is launched from the fleet's batch step, not the session's — reckon half: dispatch, resumption and producer routing through the fleet FIFO | implementable |
| 5 | `a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker` | 10 | Same section — the move: `bin/fleet-supervisor` into reckon with a `spawn` verb (lead decisions `c-20260924T145608411343`, `c-20260924T150420460935`) | implementable |
| 6 | `the-local-lane-is-the-default` | 5 | Paid-backend headroom is observed continuously and published | implementable |
| 7 | `a-worker-cannot-write-your-home` | 2 | A worker's own harness state lives in its run (CODEX_HOME fix at `27e11161`) | delivered, unmerged |

Two readings the coordinator should confirm if the wave was planned otherwise: node 1 is §2 ("the server listens before it watches"), **not** §3/§4 (the metadata index and the paint) — sections 3 and 4 are separate nodes and are not in this map; and nodes 4 and 5 are the two halves of §10, which the lead split when it decided the supervisor's home is reckon (`a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker.html`, comments at lines 715 and 718).

## Node 1 — `the-page-paints-from-an-index` §2 (the server listens before it watches)

**Measure.** `main` binds and serves first; the fleet change watch is built on a background thread, one tree at a time; a tree is covered by the reuse window until its watch is armed.

| Reuse candidate | file:line | What the node composes from it |
|---|---|---|
| `_ProjectChangeWatch` | `reckon/serve.py:99` | the per-tree watch class; its construction is the slow step, so it must move off the listen path — not be re-implemented |
| `self._watches` registry + the watch-arming loop | `reckon/serve.py:201`–`204` | the start-up loop that builds one watch per tree; this is the loop to defer to a background thread |
| `_ProjectChangeWatch(docs_dir)` per-project arm | `reckon/serve.py:2062` | the same class on the per-project path; §2's threading must not break it |
| `main(...)` | `reckon/serve.py:3059` | the served entry point the test drives; the listen/watch order is decided here |
| `ThreadingHTTPServer(...).serve_forever()` | `reckon/serve.py:3091` | the bind-and-serve call that must precede, not follow, the watch build |
| `synthesize_landed_record` / `discover_landed_records` — **not** needed | – | no: §2 changes no row-building; listed here only to mark the boundary |

**Write paths (plan-fixed).** `reckon/serve.py`; `tests/test_server_listens_before_it_watches.py`.
**Gate.** Starts the served entry point over three temporary mounts with `_ProjectChangeWatch` construction patched to 5 s/tree; asserts `/_projects/mounts.json` answers 200 within 2 s of launch and every watch is armed within 20 s. Negative control: watch built before `serve_forever` → the 2 s assertion fails.

**Depends on §3, which has landed** (`3ad8b13c`): the table may only claim "returns once its supervisor is running" because the supervisor exists.

## Node 3 — `a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker` §7 (a promoted run keeps its worker's exit)

**Measure.** `complete` in `reckon/crew/promotion.py` reads the run directory's `exit.json` when it exists and copies it verbatim under the ledger row's `worker_exit`; it never writes `exit_status`; no `exit.json` → no `worker_exit` key at HEAD, `worker_exit` does not exist anywhere in `reckon/` or `tests/` (verified: `grep -rn worker_exit reckon/ tests/` prints nothing) — so this node adds a key, it does not move one.

| Reuse candidate | file:line | What the node composes from it |
|---|---|---|
| `EXIT_RECORD_NAME = "exit.json"` | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:4766` | the canonical record name §3's supervisor writes; read it, never re-spell it |
| `complete(...)` | `reckon/crew/promotion.py:2461` | the promotion entry: where the row is composed and where the copy is added, inside the existing `_pointer_lock` region |
| `_recorded_exit_status(log_text)` | `reckon/crew/promotion.py:494` | the gate-log reader the row's `exit_status` already comes from; §7 must leave it as the gate's status, never overwrite it with the worker exit |
| `discard(...)` | `reckon/crew/promotion.py:4165` | the sibling promotion path; §7's copy must not smuggle a `worker_exit` into a discard |
| ledger row composition (the dict carrying `exit_status`) | `reckon/crew/promotion.py:~675` | the row shape the new key joins; a present-but-empty key is the defect the section names |
| the run directory resolver used by promotion | `reckon/crew/promotion.py` (via `read_pointer(run_id)` inside `complete`) | how a run id resolves to its directory; reuse it rather than composing a second path |

**Write paths (plan-fixed).** `reckon/crew/promotion.py`; `tests/test_promotion_keeps_the_worker_exit.py`.
**Gate.** Promotes a run whose `exit.json` carries `signal_name: SIGKILL`, `ended_during: working`; asserts `worker_exit` equals that object and the row's `exit_status` is the value passed to `--gate-exit-status`. Second case: no `exit.json` → no `worker_exit` key. Two negative controls (copy removed → case 1 fails; empty key when absent → case 2 fails).
**Conflict note.** `a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker` §9 (a discard removes a worktree that holds no work, not among the seven) also writes `reckon/crew/promotion.py`. If §9 is dispatched in the same wave it also writes `tests/test_discard_removes_an_empty_worktree.py`, a different test file, so the two share only the source module — serialise them.

## Node 4 — `a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker` §10, reckon half (dispatch/resumption/producer routing through the fleet FIFO)

**Measure.** With a live fleet record whose node holds the dispatching process, `_start_supervisor` writes one `spawn` line to the FIFO instead of forking, waits ≤10 s for `<run_dir>/spawned.json`, records the acknowledged pid on the pointer; no fleet record or off the fleet node → forks as today; an unacknowledged FIFO refuses leaving no pointer and no worktree. `crew watch` producers, sweep-dispatched reviews and resumptions take the same path.

| Reuse candidate | file:line | What the node composes from it |
|---|---|---|
| `_start_supervisor(spec_path, run_directory)` | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:4880` | the one function to branch: today it builds `[sys.executable, "-m", "reckon.crew.dispatch", SUPERVISOR_ENTRY, "--spec", str(spec_path)]` and `Popen`s it with `start_new_session=True`. The fleet branch writes that argv to the FIFO instead — build the argv in one place and let both lanes consume it |
| `SUPERVISOR_ENTRY = "__supervise__"` | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:4770` | the sentinel the batch step must reproduce verbatim |
| supervisor entry branch | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:1665` | the `if arguments and arguments[0] == SUPERVISOR_ENTRY:` arm; unchanged, but the proof that the argv is still a valid entry when it comes from the FIFO |
| `_supervisor_spec(...)` | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:4844` | the spec the verb must be able to run blind: today it carries `plan.argv`/`plan.cwd` (the *worker* argv). §10 requires reckon to write the *supervisor* argv into the spec as `argv` so the verb needs no reckon knowledge |
| caller of `_start_supervisor` | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:4052` | `spawned_pid = _start_supervisor(spec_path, directory)` — the single call site; the pid it records is the pointer's pid, and the fleet branch must record the *acknowledged* pid instead |
| `_spawn(...)` | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:4702` | the fork `_start_supervisor` must keep using off-fleet (the login lane is unchanged) |
| `_write_boundary_tree_snapshot(...)` | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:~4935` | the pre-spawn snapshot that runs inside the supervisor; its position is unaffected by §10, and its cost is what makes a run read as *launching* (followup `f-adrwiw-launch-flicker`) |
| `EXIT_RECORD_NAME` | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:4766` | the run-directory artifacts reconciliation reads; the same directory receives `spawned.json` |
| `dispatch_awaiting_reviews(...)` | `reckon/crew/recovery.py:1135` | the reflex that dispatches a run's review; re-exported at `reckon/crew/resumption.py:68` and called at `reckon/crew/resumption.py:1200` — this call path must route through the supervisor |
| `sweep(...)` + its `launcher` seam | `reckon/crew/resumption.py:979`, `:1008` (`launch = _spawn if launcher is None else launcher`) | the test seam that today takes the inline branch; §10 routes both through the supervisor. The seam stays for tests |
| `_resume(...)` | `reckon/crew/resumption.py:653` | the resume path that passes a launcher today; §10's second routed caller |
| `crew watch` command | `reckon/cli.py:2445` (`@crew.command(name="watch")`) | the producer launch site; `--ensure` → `ensure_watcher_service` is the durable seat path |
| `ensure_watcher_service(project)` | `reckon/crew/runs.py:2260` | the producer's systemd-unit start; the launch it performs must take the FIFO path |
| `reservation_path(project=None)` / `read_reservation(...)` | `reckon/crew/placement.py:74` / `:97` | where the fleet job is published today (per-project keyed; the keying retirement is `the-fleet-shares-one-allocation`, a different plan) |
| `reservation_alive(...)` | `reckon/crew/placement.py:181` | "is a live fleet job named" — the predicate the fleet branch needs; reuse it rather than re-probing squeue |
| `_dispatch_lane_reading(backend)` | `reckon/crew/dispatch.py:2352`, reading the key at `:2364` | precedent for how dispatch reads a published local document from the backend declaration; the fork/FIFO decision is a second such read |

**Write paths (plan-fixed).** `reckon/crew/dispatch.py`; `reckon/crew/resumption.py`; the `crew watch` launch site (`reckon/cli.py`, `reckon/crew/runs.py` on the `--ensure` path); `tests/test_dispatch_spawns_through_the_fleet.py`; and in dotfiles `bin/fleet-supervisor` (the `spawn` verb half — see node 5: dotfiles is a reference, not the destination, after the lead's move decision).
**Gate.** Five things in one test: one `spawn` line written and its pid acknowledged; no fleet record → forks; unacknowledged FIFO → refusal leaving nothing; a producer start takes the same path; a sweep-dispatched review and a resumption both reach the supervisor. On the fleet node: the worker survives `scancel` on the *step id alone*, log names step and job, worker's parent is the batch step. Negative control: FIFO path bypassed → the worker dies with its step.
**Ordering.** This node and node 5 are two halves of one protocol (FIFO line format, `spawned.json` acknowledgement). Land node 5's verb and this routing node against the same agreed protocol; the plan's test stub can be written first (temporary runtime dir, FIFO, stub batch-step reader).

## Node 6 — `the-local-lane-is-the-default` §5 (paid-backend headroom published continuously)

**Measure.** One small observer publishes one document per account (e.g. `~/public/reckon/paid-lanes.json`) carrying, per window (five-hour and seven-day): utilisation, reset time, burn multiple, projected exhaustion, source, `observed_at`; it reconciles sources by recency and reports which it used; preflight and dispatch read that document; one stale field reports stale for that field alone, and one missing precondition no longer turns every field `unknown`; refreshed ≥ every 5 minutes, with a reading for each of the three backends.

**Write paths.** The plan names none. **Derived, for the coordinator to fix before dispatch:** the observer module (mirroring the local lane's publisher; a new module or `reckon/crew/lane_document.py` for the shared schema), `reckon/budget.py` (preflight reads the document), the dispatch-side read if §5's "dispatch read that document" is taken literally (`reckon/crew/dispatch.py` — **conflicts with node 4**), a publisher entry point (CLI verb or systemd timer), and a test. **Adjacent, not in this wave:** §6 (state grading and the pushed line) reads this document; §7 (one follower per session) is independent.
**Precedent to copy.** The stale-hold guidance in the user-global AGENTS.md — fence on `unifiedWindows.<period>.utilization` against its own `resetsAt`, never the account-level overage figure — is the semantic the document must preserve; the per-field staleness rule in §5 is what makes it work.

## Node 7 — `a-worker-cannot-write-your-home` §2 (a worker's own harness state lives in its run)

**Measure.** `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` = `<run_dir>/harness`; `CODEX_HOME` set the same way with `auth.json` bound read-only; transcript and session files land in the run directory and `crew resume` finds them there; nothing under the real `~/.claude` changes; a fenced codex worker leaves `~/.codex/auth.json` unchanged.

**This work is delivered and deliberately unmerged.** The fence and harness home are on commits reachable from `27e11161` — the combined head — and **not** from HEAD (`git merge-base --is-ancestor c72c460e HEAD` fails; no ref contains them). The node's first act is to merge at `27e11161` after the CODEX_HOME repair; the map's citations for the fence code therefore resolve at `27e11161`, not at HEAD.

**Wave interaction (important).** Merging this flips the fence on for **every** worker in **every** session. Any other node running concurrently with unmerged peer work in a sibling worktree will find that tree read-only. Land this one when no other node of the wave holds uncommitted work in another worktree, and say so in the wave's ordering.
